L49: ## 1 Executive Summary
L50: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L51: Tebentafusp-tebn (referred to as tebentafusp) is a bispecific gp100 peptide-HLA-directed CD3 T cell engager. The tumor cell receptor arm of tebentafusp binds to a gp100 peptide presented by human leukocyte antigen-A*02:01 (HLA-A*02:01) on the cell surface of uveal melanoma tumor cells. Tebentafusp exerts its anti-tumor effect by redirecting T cells to attack gp100-expressing tumor cells.
L52: At the time of submission of the current BLA 761228 on June 23, 2021, tebentafusp was not yet approved for any indication in the United States. The Applicant's proposed indication at the time of BLA submission was as follows:
L53: Tebentafusp is a bispecific gp100-(b) (4) T cell (b) (4) for the treatment of HLA-A*02:01-positive adult patients with unresectable or metastatic uveal melanoma
L54: The proposed dosing regimen is 20 mcg on Day 1, 30 mcg on Day 8, 68 mcg on Day 15, and 68 mcg once every week thereafter, via intravenous infusion over 15-20 minutes.
L55: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L56: The review team has determined that the results of Study 202, an open-label, randomized, multi-center study in 378 adult HLA-A*02:01-positive patients with unresectable or metastatic uveal melanoma (mUM) demonstrate a statistically significant and clinically meaningful improvement in overall survival (OS) in patients treated with tebentafusp. This trial is ongoing and has enrolled patients with histologically or cytologically confirmed mUM who had not received prior systemic therapy in the metastatic setting or prior regional liver-directed therapy. Patients were randomized 2:1 to receive tebentafusp or investigator's choice of therapy (dacarbazine, ipilimumab, or pembrolizumab) until radiographic progression (except for patients receiving ipilimumab), unacceptable toxicity, investigator decision, or patient withdrawal of consent. The primary study endpoint was overall survival (OS), and key secondary endpoint was investigator-assessed progression free survival (PFS) per RECIST 1.1.
L57: The data submitted by the Applicant has provided substantial evidence for the effectiveness of tebentafusp administered at 20 mcg on Day 1, 30 mcg on Day 8, 68 mcg on Day 15, and 68 mcg once every week thereafter for the treatment of HLA-A*02:01-positive adult patients with unresectable or metastatic uveal melanoma and supports regular approval for this indication. This conclusion is based on results from Study 202 which demonstrates a clinically and statistically significant improvement in OS in patients who received tebentafusp compared to
L58: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761228
## Kimmtrak (tebentafusp)
L59: those who received investigator's choice of therapy (HR of 0.51 [95% CI: 0.37, 0.71], p &lt; 0.0001).
L60: The safety of tebentafusp has been adequately characterized by data submitted from Study 202 and by supportive data submitted from other studies in the tebentafusp development program. The review team believes that the magnitude of improvement in OS provides a benefit that outweighs the risks associated with systemic treatment with tebentafusp for this patient population with no FDA approved therapies for mUM.
L61: Therefore the review team recommends granting regular approval to tebentafusp for the treatment of HLA-A*02:01-positive adult patients with unresectable or metastatic uveal melanoma.
L62: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761228
## Kimmtrak (tebentafusp)
L63: ## 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)
L64: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L65: Uveal melanoma is a rare subset that accounts for &lt;5% of all cases of melanoma in the United States but represents the most commonly occurring intraocular malignancy in adults (Sayan 2020, Kaliki 2017). There are no FDA approved therapies for the treatment of uveal melanoma. Patients with uveal melanoma who have systemic disease are treated with therapies approved for cutaneous melanoma, which have demonstrated limited efficacy in this population. In the metastatic uveal melanoma setting, the National Comprehensive Cancer Network (NCCN) guidelines version 2.2021 recommends participation in a clinical trial as the preferred management option with consideration of monotherapy or combination regimen consisting of liver-directed therapies (e.g. ablative procedures, embolization, isolation of perfusion, resection or radiation), systemic therapies (e.g. immune-oncology agents, cytotoxic regimens or trametinib), resection or radiation treatment for limited or symptomatic extrahepatic disease, or palliative care. Despite responses as high as 90% to treatment options for localized uveal melanoma, as many as 50% of patients will develop metastatic disease (Nichols 2016). The prognosis in patients who develop metastatic uveal melanoma is very poor with median overall survival less than 6 months (Rantala 2019).
L66: The Applicant has submitted data from Study 202 to support the approval of tebentafusp for the treatment of HLA-A*02:01-positive adult patients with unresectable or metastatic uveal melanoma. A favorable benefit-risk assessment has been established for tebentafusp based on demonstration of a clinically meaningful and statistically significant improvement in OS in patients who received tebentafusp compared to those who received Investigator's choice of therapy which include dacarbazine, pembrolizumab or ipilimumab (HR of 0.51 [95% CI: 0.37, 0.71], p &lt; 0.0001).
L67: The data submitted to support the safety review of tebentafusp is adequate to characterize toxicity in patients with uveal melanoma. The toxicity profile of tebentafusp in patients with HLA-A*02:01 unresectable or metastatic uveal melanoma was comparable with the toxicity observed in patients treated with Investigator's choice of therapy in Study 202. Although there was a slightly higher incidence of SAEs observed in patients treated with tebentafusp, overall tebentafusp appeared to be well tolerated. Fatal adverse events and permanent treatment discontinuation in patients on tebentafusp are rare. Adverse events of special interest (AESIs) were identified in patients treated with tebentafusp. These AESI included cytokine release syndrome (CRS), skin reactions (which includes the subcategories of rash, pruritis, pigment change, erythema, edema, dry skin, and other changes) and elevated hepatic enzymes. Risk minimization strategies have been instituted via
L68: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761228
## Kimmtrak (tebentafusp)
L69: current management guidelines included in the product labeling and a Medication Guide. In addition, health care providers that will be responsible for prescribing or managing patients on tebentafusp will receive a “Dear Health Care Provider” letter to increase awareness of the risk of CRS, which can be serious or fatal, associated with tebentafusp.
L70: The review team recommends that tebentafusp be approved for the treatment of patients with HLA-A*02:01-positive unresectable or metastatic uveal melanoma.
L71: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	Uveal melanoma is a rare subset of melanoma and accounts for <5% of all cases of melanoma in the United States.
The incidence of uveal melanoma is estimated to be 4 to 5 cases per million per year (Sayan 2020; Kaliki 2017).
Localized disease may be treated with radiation or surgery, however almost half of patients will develop systemic disease (Sayan 2020).
Uveal melanoma is a serious and life-threatening condition. Patients diagnosed with metastatic disease have a poor prognosis with median survival <6 months (Rantala 2019).
Current Treatment Options	Non-metastatic disease can be treated with radiation or surgery depending on the size of the lesion.
Patients with advanced and metastatic uveal melanoma are encouraged to participate in clinical trials. Other options for management include a combination approach liver-directed therapies, systemic therapies, and resection or radiation for limited or symptomatic extrahepatic disease (NCCN 2021).
Uveal melanoma is treated with the same systemic therapies used for the management of cutaneous melanoma; however these therapies have shown limited benefit in patients with uveal melanoma.
There are no approved therapies specifically for uveal melanoma and no effective therapy options once disease has metastasized. Given the poor prognosis, there remains an unmet medical need.
L72: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761228
## Kimmtrak (tebentafusp)
L73: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Benefit	In Study 202, tebentafusp demonstrated a clinically and statistically significant improvement in OS compared to investigator choice of dacarbazine, pembrolizumab or ipilimumab (HR of 0.51 [95% CI: 0.37, 0.71], p < 0.0001).
The study met its primary objective with tebentafusp demonstrating superiority over investigator's choice therapy. OS is the preferred endpoint to demonstrate clinically meaningfulness and effectiveness of new therapeutics across oncology indications.
Risk and Risk Management	The safety profile of tebentafusp for the treatment of HLA-A*02:01-positive metastatic or unresectable uveal melanoma appears acceptable for a therapy used for the treatment of a serious and life-threatening disease.
Rates of AEs were comparable (to slightly higher) on the tebentafusp arm with those observed on the Investigator's Choice arm. Fatal adverse events and permanent treatment discontinuation in patients on tebentafusp are rare.
Tebentafusp has been associated with adverse events of special interest (AESIs) which include cytokine release syndrome, skin reactions, and elevated liver enzymes.
The safety data submitted was sufficient to characterize the toxicity profile of tebentafusp and the risks are considered acceptable in the context of the clinical efficacy in the advanced or metastatic disease setting. The USPI has been appropriately amended to provide guidance for the management of AESIs, Routine pharmacovigilance for late toxicities is warranted.

L81: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L82: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L83: ## The Applicant's Position:
L84: Uveal melanoma is a rare and highly malignant subset of melanoma, representing &lt; 5% of all melanoma cases in the United States (US; McLaughlin, 2005). Uveal melanoma is the most frequent primary intraocular malignancy of the adult eye (~85%; Patel, 2011; Maio, 2013). The incidence varies by geography, race, and age, ranging from 5.3 to 10.9 cases per million (Singh, 2011). Uveal melanoma arises exclusively from melanocytes of the uvea, and it is biologically, clinically, and genetically distinct from cutaneous melanoma (CM; Jager, 2020). *BRAF* and *NRAS* mutations dominate the landscape in CM, whereas mutations in guanine nucleotide binding protein, q polypeptide, and alpha 11 dominate in UM (Shoushtari, 2014). The mode of disease spread is distinct between the 2 diseases, with hematogenous spread being most common in UM while lymphatic predominates in CM. Moreover, UM has a highly immunosuppressive tumor microenvironment (Jager, 2020; Rothermel, 2016). Cutaneous melanoma has one of the highest tumor mutational burdens (TMB), whereas the TMB of UM is among the lowest of all cancers (Lee, 2019; Yarchoan, 2017). The low TMB in UM results in few neoantigens, which limits natural antitumor immunity (Violanti, 2019), making UM less sensitive to immune checkpoint inhibitors than CM.
L85: Uveal melanoma is a life-threatening disease with no effective therapy once it metastasizes. Despite local therapy (radiation and surgery), up to 50% of patients with UM develop systemic metastases, predominantly to the liver (~90% of patients) and less commonly to the lungs and bones (Nathan, 2015). Once patients develop metastatic UM (mUM), the prognosis and outcomes are dismal, with a median survival of ≤ 12 months (Rantala, 2019; Khoja, 2019). Over the past 40 years, there has been no significant improvement in survival for patients with mUM.
L86: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L87: FDA agrees with the Applicant's characterization and assessment of the disease.
L88: ## 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L89: ## The Applicant's Position:
L90: No products are formally approved by FDA for the treatment of metastatic uveal melanoma.
L91: ## Current Treatment Landscape for Uveal Melanoma
L92: In contrast to other melanomas, particularly CM, current treatment options for mUM are limited and deemed ineffective. Metastatic UM responds poorly to cytotoxic chemotherapy, radiotherapy, and immunotherapy (Buder, 2013; Pereira, 2013). No systemic or local therapies are approved specifically for the treatment of mUM (Carvajal, 2014; Luke, 2013; Maio, 2013). Treatments introduced for CM over the past decade have not significantly benefited patients with UM, reflecting the distinct biology, genetics, and clinical course of UM. The standard of care for mUM is enrollment in a clinical trial (Carvajal, 2017). When clinical trials are not available, therapies for advanced CM are used.
L93: Representative multicenter clinical studies or case series of at least 20 patients with mUM who received available therapies are summarized in **Error! Reference source not found.** Studies with single-agent chemotherapy arms showed a median overall survival (OS) of approximately 10 months (Buder, 2013). Single-agent immune checkpoint inhibitors (anti-cytotoxic T-lymphocyte associated antigen-4 [CTLA-4] or anti-programmed cell death-1 [PD-1]) exhibited median OS similar to that of cytotoxic therapy in mUM (approximately 6 to 10 months). These data are generally consistent with a global meta-analysis that included &gt; 900 patients in Phase 2 trials in mUM, demonstrating a median OS of approximately 9 months and 1-year OS rate of 43% across treatment modalities and lines of therapy in the metastatic setting (Khoja, 2019). In a meta-analysis of &gt; 2400 first line (1L) mUM patients, median OS was about 12 months with a 1-year OS rate of 52% (Rantala, 2019). Although the combination of anti-CTLA-4 and anti-PD-1 has a numerically higher objective response rate (ORR), this combination has a 1-year OS rate similar to checkpoint monotherapy (Pelster, 2021; Piulats, 2021). Together, these data suggest that a new treatment option with different mechanism of action (MoA) to those used for other melanomas is essential to improve outcomes in mUM, a serious and life-threatening disease.

L126: ## 4 Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety
L127: ## 4.1. Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
L128: The Division of Oncology 3 consulted OSI to discuss an audit of overall trial conduct. Four clinical investigators were selected for audit. These were Dr. Hassel (Site 3504), Dr. Piperno-Neumann (Site 6101), Dr. Rutkowski (Site 6401), and Dr. Kirkwood (Site 8705) The Applicant, Immunocore LLC., was also inspected as this is the first marketing application from the Applicant and a new molecular entity. All inspections were conducted onsite. During the inspection of Site 6401 it was noted that updates to the electronic database involving efficacy and safety data had been made after the database lock for the submitted application which were reportedly to be due to limited site resources affecting their ability to complete data cleaning prior to the submission. There was one change in the primary end point of overall survival (OS) for the control arm (USUBID 6401-(b) (6)). A request for information dated November 10, 2021, was sent to the Applicant to determine the extent of changes impacting the primary efficacy endpoint of OS and secondary efficacy endpoints of PFS and BOR across all sites. The Applicant reported that no changes to the OS endpoint occurred in any other patient besides 6401-(b) (6). Four additional patients treated at 4 different sites (other than 6401) had a change impacting either PFS and/or BOR. After FDA review, it was determined that these updates were unlikely to change the analysis of OS or PFS or to have a significant impact on interpretation of the study results. The other inspectional findings verified the Applicant's reported clinical data with source records at these study sites. No significant GCP compliance deviations were observed at these sites. Based on the inspectional findings, the OSI review team considers the submitted data from this study reliable for this NDA and the respective indication.

L136: ## 5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L137: ## 5.1. Executive Summary
L138: Tebentafusp (IMCgp100) is a bispecific fusion protein that consists of a soluble T cell receptor (TCR) fused to an anti-cluster of differentiation 3 (anti-CD3) single-chain variable fragment (scFv). The TCR domain binds a gp100 peptide (peptide fragment derived from the melanoma associated antigen glycoprotein 100, position 280-288, YLEPGPVTA):human leukocyte antigen (HLA)-A*02:01 complex. Glycoprotein 100 (gp100) is a type 1 transmembrane glycoprotein that is enriched in melanosomes. The established pharmacologic class (EPC) is bispecific gp100 peptide-HLA-directed CD3 T-cell engager.
L139: In an in vitro assay, tebentafusp bound the gp100 peptide:HLA-A*02:01 complex with a mean K_D value of 24 pM. Tebentafusp bound CD3 with a mean K_D value of 38 nM. Average bifunctional binding of tebentafusp to gp100 peptide-HLA-A*02:01 and soluble CD3 was 88%. Tebentafusp exhibited binding specificity towards the HLA-A*02:01 subtype, with EC_{50} values ranging from ~30 to 60 pM for HLA-A*02:01-positive cell lines. Comparatively, tebentafusp bound other HLA-A subtypes with EC_{50} values of \ge 166 pM. In HLA-A*02:01-positive cutaneous and uveal melanoma cell lines co-incubated with peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs), tebentafusp treatment led to a dose-dependent increase in IFN\gamma release with EC_{50} values ranging from 0.89 to 121 pM. T cell activation by tebentafusp increased cytokines IFN\gamma, TNF-\alpha, IL-6, IL-2, and MIP-1\beta. In vivo, the anti-tumor activity of tebentafusp was evaluated in mice engrafted with HLA-A*02:01/gp100-positive Mel526 human melanoma cancer cells mixed with PBMCs isolated from human whole blood. In this study, administration of 5 doses of tebentafusp resulted in tumor growth inhibition compared to controls.
L140: There are no pharmacologically relevant animal species to evaluate tebentafusp toxicity. In vitro, the scFv portion of tebentafusp bound to T cells in human PBMCs but did not bind PBMCs from mouse or monkey. Tebentafusp was evaluated in a series of in vitro studies to characterize potential cross-reactivity in 35 normal human tissues by immunohistochemistry (IHC) and the potential for organ-specific toxicities in functional assays using 13 normal human tissues. Tebentafusp did not show off-target/cross-reactivity in tissues outside of melanocytes, and alloreactivity and platelet activation were not observed.
L141: Genotoxicity and carcinogenicity studies were not warranted.
L142: Tebentafusp was not evaluated in embryofetal toxicity studies. In the reproductive toxicity assessment provided by the Applicant, the following points are considered: (1) gp100 is expressed embryonically in melanocytes found in the skin, inner ear, and eyes by 7 weeks of gestation; (2) MHC Class I classical proteins (including HLA-A*02:01) and transporter-associated-with-antigen-processing (TAP) proteins required for presentation of antigen on the cell surface are expressed early in embryonic development; and (3) CD3-positive T cells begin to populate the periphery and are capable of being activated by around 12 weeks of gestation.
L143: Fusion proteins may cross the placenta. In an embryo-fetal development study in mice, a murine surrogate of blinatumomab, which has a similar molecular weight and mechanism of action as tebentafusp, crossed the placental barrier and fetal exposure occurred at pharmacologically active concentrations.
L144: Based on its size and mechanism of action, tebentafusp may cross the placenta and cause fetal harm, particularly the skin, inner ear, and eye, if the developing fetus is exposed to tebentafusp.
L145: There are no approvability issues from a pharmacology/toxicology perspective.

L149: ## 5.3. Pharmacology
L150: ## Primary pharmacology
L151: ## In vitro studies
L152: Surface plasmon resonance (SPR) was used to assess binding (Study IMC 1022). The mean K_D value of tebentafusp for the gp100 peptide-HLA-A*02:01 complex was 24 pM. The mean K_D value of tebentafusp for CD3 was 38 nM. When SPR was used to evaluate the bifunctional binding of tebentafusp, 88% of gp100 peptide-HLA-A*02:01 complex-bound tebentafusp bound soluble CD3.
L153: The Applicant also used SPR to assess the ability of tebentafusp to bind the gp100 peptide complexed with other HLA-A2 subtypes (HLA-A*02:02, 03, 05, 06, and 07; Study IMC 1040). In this study, tebentafusp was affinity enhanced to bind a heteroclitic (e.g., sequence-modified) version of the gp100 peptide, in which the C-terminal amino acid was substituted from an alanine to a valine to provide binding stability on HLA-A*02. As shown in Table 3, the binding of IMCgp100 (tebentafusp) was reduced with other A2 subtypes as compared to HLA-A*02:01, as measured by relative residence half-life values.

L157: The Applicant conducted cellular assays to support the HLA-A*02 subtype binding data. Briefly, plasmids encoding the subtypes HLA-A*02:01, HLA-A*02:02, HLA-A*02:04, HLA-A*02:05, and HLA-A*02:06 were generated under the control of a human promoter. These plasmids were transduced into the SK-Mel28 HLA-A*02-negative cutaneous melanoma cell line and cells were incubated overnight with increasing concentrations of tebentafusp in the presence of PBMCs. T cell activation was assessed via IFN\gamma production ELISPOT (Study IMC 1061).

L161: The Applicant assessed the ability of tebentafusp to inhibit the proliferation of tumor cells that are positive for HLA-A*02:01, including the HLA-A*02:01-positive cutaneous melanoma (CM) cell line, Mel624, and the uveal melanoma (UM) cell lines, mel-202 and 92-1 (Study IMC 1061). Due to limited cell line availability for UM, the two HLA-A*02:01-negative cell lines (92-1 and mel-202) were transduced with a construct to express HLA-A*02:01 and \beta2M under the control of a human promoter to allow for physiological levels of HLA-A*02:01 expression. Tumor cells were incubated with non-tumor specific PBMCs from healthy human donors and increasing concentrations of tebentafusp. T cell activation was determined by measuring IFN\gamma release (ELISPOT), while tumor cell killing was determined by the IncuCyte cell killing assay. Briefly, NucView is a DNA dye-based reagent that is membrane permeable and does not fluoresce unless bound to DNA. In its native state, NucView contains a substrate that prevents binding to DNA. However in the presence of active caspase-3/7, this substrate is cleaved to release the dye to bind DNA. Upon binding to DNA, the dye will emit fluorescence at 488 nm counts are the number of objects that are fluorescing per unit area.
L162: Data from the IFN\gamma ELISPOT assay (representative data from one PMBC donor) is shown in Figure 1. Incubation of tumor cell lines with PMBCs and tebentafusp led to a dose-dependent increase in IFN\gamma release, indicating a tebentafusp-mediated redirected PMBC response against the HLA-A*02:01-positive tumor cell lines. The EC50 values for Mel624, mel-202 HLA-A*201/\beta2M, and 92-1 HLA-A*201/\beta2M were 121, 0.89, and 53 pM, respectively.
L163: **Figure 1: PMBCs release IFN\gamma following incubation with tebentafusp (IMCgp100) and HLA-A*02:01 positive tumor cell lines**
L164: (Source: Applicant figure reproduced from Study IMC 1061)
L165: Representative data from the IncuCyte killing assay is shown in Figure 2. The graphs show the accumulation of apoptotic target cells over a 47 hour time course. Uveal melanoma cells were killed with faster kinetics than cutaneous melanoma cells at doses as low as 10 pM tebentafusp.
L166: Figure 2: Cell kill kinetics of tebentafusp
L167: (Source: Applicant figure reproduced from Study IMC 1061)
L168: Similar IFN\gamma and T cell killing data were seen when antigen-positive cutaneous melanoma cell lines were incubated with tebentafusp in the presence of CD4+ and CD8+ T cells from healthy human donors as well as donor PBMCs from patients with stage 4 melanoma (Study IMC 1014, IMC 1015, IMC 1017, IMC 1020).
L169: The Applicant performed an assay to determine if T cell activation by tebentafusp induced release of proinflammatory cytokines (Study IMC 1020). In this study, tebentafusp was incubated with HLA-A*02:01-positive Mel526 CM cells and HLA-A*02:01-negative A375 melanoma cells co-cultured with PBMCs as effector cells. Cytokine release was measured via the Luminex assay. HLA-A*02:01-positive Mel526 cells incubated overnight with 1 nM tebentafusp showed release of proinflammatory cytokines, while HLA-A*02:01-negative A375 cell lines did not (Figure 3).
L170: **Figure 3: Tebentafusp (IMCgp100)-mediated proinflammatory cytokine release by HLA-A*02:01-positive tumor cells**
L171: (Source: Applicant figure reproduced from Study IMC 1020)
L172: Comparable results were seen in co-culture assay with PMBCs isolated from patients with cutaneous melanoma (Study IMC 1046).
L173: Tebentafusp exhibits its pharmacologic activity through the redirected activation of CD3+ T cells. To test whether the activity of tebentafusp could be blocked by corticosteroids, tebentafusp-mediated T cell activation assays (IFN\gamma ELISPOT) were performed (Study IMC 1018). Mel526 cells were co-incubated with CD8+ T cells and 1 nM tebentafusp, with increasing concentrations of hydrocortisone. As shown in Figure 4, tebentafusp-mediated T cell activity (via measurement of IFN\gamma release) was inhibited by hydrocortisone in a dose-dependent manner.
L174: Figure 4: Hydrocortisone inhibition of CD8+ T cell activation by tebentafusp
L175: (Source: Applicant figure reproduced from Study IMC 1018)
L176: Tebentafusp redirects T cell activity through its anti-CD3 scFv effector domain. The Applicant performed studies to evaluate the kinetics of tebentafusp T cell redirecting capacity and to analyze the subsets of T cells that are activated. To determine the effects of tebentafusp on activation of specific T cell subsets, polychromatic flow cytometry was performed on T cells using gp100 peptide-pulsed PBMCs as target cells. The phenotypic profile of CD8+ T cells activated with tebentafusp is shown in Figure 5 (tebentafusp at a concentration of \sim 10^{-11} M in the presence of gp100 peptide-pulsed PBMC target cells). Activated cells undergoing degranulation are depicted in green (CD107a staining). The major T cell populations activated in these experiments were the effector memory (T_{EM}) and central memory (T_{CM}) subsets as determined by CD45RO and CD27 staining.
L177: Figure 5: Tebentafusp mediated activation of T_{EM} and T_{CM} subsets
L178: (Source: Applicant figure reproduced from Study IMC 1027)
L179: To determine the tebentafusp killing kinetics sustained by PBMCs, CD8+, and CD4+ T cell subpopulations, Mel526 cells were treated with a clinically relevant concentration of
L180: tebentafusp (82 pM), co-cultured with effector cell subsets, and cell killing was assessed using the IncuCyte platform (Study IMC 1045). As shown in Figure 6, apoptotic events using PBMCs begin around 22 hours with a peak at ~48 hours.
L181: **Figure 6: Tebentafusp (IMCgp100) redirected PBMC killing kinetics**
L182: (Source: Applicant figure reproduced from Study IMC 1045)
L183: In this assay, tebentafusp also redirected CD8+ and CD4+ T cells to kill gp100-positive melanoma cells. CD8+ cell subset kinetics are shown in Figure 7A and CD4+ cell subset kinetics are shown in Figure 7B. Killing by tebentafusp-redirected CD4+ T cells was delayed compared to CD8+ T cells but with similar total activity. In both CD4+ and CD8+ T cells, TEM and TEMRA (effector memory T cells re-expressing CD45RA) subsets were the most effective and fastest at mediating tumor cell killing.
L184: **Figure 7: Tebentafusp redirected CD4+ and CD8+ T cell killing kinetics**
L185: (Source: Applicant figure reproduced from Study IMC 1045)
L186: The Applicant next assessed the threshold of epitope density necessary for tebentafusp-mediated T cell activation and whether the activity of tebentafusp was epitope density-dependent (Study IMC 1030). Briefly, the TCR domain of tebentafusp was tagged with biotin to allow a fluorescently labeled streptavidin to detect a single TCR-peptide:HLA interaction. Microscopy was used to quantify the number of gp100 antigens presented on CM cell lines and T2 cells (a lymphoma-derived cell line that expresses low levels of HLA-A2) that had been exogenously pulsed with increasing levels of the target gp100 peptide. To determine the relationship between the level of gp100 antigen presentation on target cells and tebentafusp-mediated T cell activation, the T2 cells were pulsed with increasing concentrations of gp100 antigen in the presence of 0.1 nM tebentafusp and CD 8+ T cells. IFN\gamma ELISPOT assays were then used to measure T cell activation.
L187: CM cell lines Mel526 and Mel624 exhibited an average of 33 epitopes and 30 epitopes, respectively (data not shown). In the T2 cells incubated with tebentafusp and the target gp100 peptide epitope, IFN\gamma release was observed starting at \sim100 pM peptide and reached its maximum between 1 and 10 nM of target gp100 peptide. These results suggest that T cell activation is correlated with epitope density. According to the Applicant, these results correspond to a minimum of \sim5 epitopes per cell for T cell activation, with maximum activation observed beginning at a concentration of 10^{-9} M gp100 peptide, corresponding to approximately 18 epitopes per cell.
L188: Figure 8: Tebentafusp-mediated T cell activation correlates with epitope density
L189: (Source: Applicant figure reproduced from Study IMC 1030)
L190: The Applicant performed a study to determine if the scFv portion of tebentafusp (UCHT1) binds to T cells in human, mouse, and monkey PBMCs (Study IMC 1029). Briefly, PBMCs were isolated from blood from human donors, cynomolgus monkeys, and mice (strain unspecified). Samples were stained with UCHT1-his scFv protein, an IgG1 PE (isotype control), and species specific anti-CD3 antibodies. Binding was assessed via flow cytometry. Comparison of staining of PBMCs from human, monkey, and mouse indicated that UCHT1 binds to CD3 on human T cells only (Figure 9).
L191: **Figure 9: Tebentafusp binding to CD3 on human, monkey, and mouse T cells**
L192: (Source: Applicant figure reproduced from Study IMC 1029)
L193: ## In vivo studies
L194: To evaluate the anti-cancer activity of tebentafusp, HLA-A*02:01/gp100-positive Mel526 human melanoma cancer cells mixed with PBMCs isolated from human whole blood were engrafted subcutaneously into female Beige/severe combined immune deficiency (SCID) mice on Day 0 (Study CAM 018). On Days 1 to 5, tebentafusp was administered to the mice via intravenous tail injection once daily, at doses ranging from 0.004 to 0.1 mg/kg. Control animals received vehicle or a molecule that consisted of a TCR recognizing the HLA-A*02:01 presenting an HIV-GAG peptide fused to an anti-CD3 scFv. Tumor size was assessed using caliper measurements three times weekly. As shown in Figure 10, tebentafusp inhibited tumor growth compared to controls.
L195: **Figure 10: Tumor volume of tebentafusp treated SCID mice engrafted with human melanoma cells and PBMCs**

L198: ## Secondary Pharmacology
L199: ## The Applicant's Position:
L200: There is no relevant species in which tebentafusp pharmacology can be tested. Therefore, extensive in vitro analysis of secondary pharmacodynamics was performed to characterize both on-target/off-tumor and off-target/off-tumor effects of tebentafusp using a range of functional activity assays. The results of these can be summarized as follows:
L201: - **Assessment in normal cells:** In vitro assessments of redirected T cell activity were performed with tebentafusp in the presence of a range of normal cells from 13 tissue types. These studies showed no reactivity of tebentafusp against normal cells at \le 1 nM, except for epidermal melanocytes. Tebentafusp demonstrated reactivity against melanocyte lots at concentrations between 10 to 100 pM (10-100\times MABEL). This activity was deemed to be on-target due to the confirmation of gp100 transcript and protein expression in the reactive cell lots, which is consistent with reports of gp100 expression in melanocytes. Given the low doses required to elicit an on-target on-tumor response, tebentafusp showed a window between normal cells and the reference
L202: CM or UM cell line tested. This activity against melanocytes was not changed when tebentafusp was combined with durvalumab and/or tremelimumab. Tebentafusp demonstrated broad loss of specificity for the target pHLA complex from 1 nM (1,000\times MABEL), where consistent evidence of T cell activation (IFN\gamma release) and/or redirected killing (GrB release) was observed across the different normal cells tested.
L203: **Alloreactivity:** Most human TCRs only recognize and bind effectively to self-HLA molecules loaded with peptides. A small proportion (less than 10%) of TCRs have the ability to recognize non-self HLA molecules and can induce allorejection. This phenomenon is termed alloreactivity (Murphy, 2016). Tebentafusp was specifically engineered and selected for high affinity binding to the HLA-A*02:01-gp100 peptide complex. Therefore, binding to a non-HLA-A*02 Class I molecule is unlikely to occur or, if it does, would occur at much reduced affinity than that for the target HLA-A*02 molecule. Alloreactivity was assessed by IFN\gamma in ELISPOT in a panel of Epstein-Barr virus-transformed B cell lines representing a large proportion of the target market Class 1 HLA genotypes, as targets, in the presence of tebentafusp. These studies were non-GLP. These studies were non-GLP. Tebentafusp demonstrated no evidence of alloreactivity when tested against panels of cells displaying other Class I HLA types (inclusive of rare alleles).
L204: **Activity in whole blood and potential for cytokine release syndrome:** The potential for cytokine release syndrome (CRS) was assessed in blood from 6 healthy HLA-A*02:01-positive donors incubated with increasing concentrations of tebentafusp, and samples were analyzed for evidence of cytokine release by multiplex. These studies were non-GLP. Tebentafusp did not show evidence for increased release of 5 key cytokines in the presence of whole blood at &lt; 1 nM. Combining tebentafusp with durvalumab and/or tremelimumab did not enhance tebentafusp-mediated cytokine release in whole blood.
L205: **Platelet activity:** Given that platelets have been reported to have very high levels of HLA on their surface (Rowley, 2011; Chapman, 2012), they are a potential target for cross-reactivity of the TCR portion of tebentafusp. Since platelets play a key role in hemostasis, tebentafusp was examined in platelet-activation assays. Platelet activation assays were performed with whole blood from 6 healthy HLA-A*02:01-positive donors and samples were assessed by flow cytometry for the presence of the platelet activation marker CD62P. Additionally, to assess the ability of tebentafusp to interfere with blood-clotting, blood from one healthy HLA-A*02:01-positive donor was incubated with increasing concentrations of tebentafusp and the time to clotting was measured. These studies were non-GLP. Tebentafusp did not give any evidence of adversely affecting platelet activation or function.
L206: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L207: The Applicant performed a number of functional cross-reactivity experiments to determine if there is a concentration of IMCgp100 at which a loss of target specificity is observed. Of note, at the recommended clinical dose, the steady-state Cmax of IMCgp100 is 13 ng/mL and AUC0-7d is 4.6 ng\cdotday/mL.
L208: - Assessment in normal cells: To determine potential reactivity of tebentafusp, levels of cytokine (IFN\gamma) or enzyme (GrB) released by redirected effector cells from PBMCs or purified CD8+ T cells were examined in a cell panel from 13 normal human tissues in the presence of increasing concentrations of tebentafusp up to 10 nM (Study IMC 1001-IMC 1013). FDA agrees with the Applicant's position with respect to melanocyte findings. Additionally, tebentafusp showed activity at &lt;1 nM in 1 of 5 donors when tested on human astrocytes (Study IMC 1001). The Applicant states: "*Based on these [astrocyte and melanocyte] data, skin rash and potentially vitiligo were expected as the primary TCR mediated on-target, off-tumor toxicities.*"
- Alloreactivity: FDA agrees. The tested concentrations were 1 nM tebentafusp (Study IMC 1021), 200 pM (Study IMC 1057).
- Activity in whole blood and potential for cytokine release syndrome: In some samples, IL-6, TNF\alpha, and IFN\gamma were detectable in whole blood at concentrations of 10 and 100 nM tebentafusp (Study IMC 1023). Levels were 2 to 10-fold below the levels of cytokine release observed with positive controls.
- Platelet activity: FDA agrees.
- Impurity Assessment: IMCgp100 contains an identified active impurity (IMCgp100 +scFv fragment), typically at a level of &lt; 1% when tested across multiple batches. The Applicant performed in vitro experiments to assess the impact (Module 4.2.1.2; Study IMCgp100-IMC 1058) on specificity of a batch of tebentafusp spiked with an artificially high (~6.7%) level of the IMCgp100 (+scFv fragment) impurity. Briefly, GMP reference standard batch of tebentafusp was compared with the spiked sample and assessed for specificity by screening for redirected T cell activity by IFN\gamma release (IFN\gamma ELISpot) with antigen positive and antigen negative normal cells derived from high risk tissue types and for cytokine release in whole blood (Study IMC 1058).
L209: Higher levels of IFN\gamma and TNF\alpha release were detected at 0.2 – 1 nM of the tebentafusp spiked sample when compared to non-spiked tebentafusp. This data indicates that high levels of impurity may alter the activity of tebentafusp.
L210: ## Safety Pharmacology
L211: ## The Applicant's Position:
L212: Tebentafusp can only recognize human HLA; therefore, all nonclinical toxicology studies were conducted in vitro. Therefore, no specific safety pharmacology studies were conducted.
L213: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L214: FDA agrees.
L215: ## 5.4. ADME/PK
L216: ## The Applicant's Position:
L217: Tebentafusp can only recognize human HLA; therefore, all nonclinical toxicology studies were conducted in vitro. Therefore, conventional in vivo animal models used to evaluate drug distribution, bioavailability, mechanism(s) of elimination, and potential risks of specific tissue toxicity are considered limited. Nevertheless, the nonclinical pharmacokinetics (PK) of tebentafusp were evaluated in four animal studies: non tumor-bearing and tumor-bearing severe combined immune deficiency (SCID) mice, C57BL/6 mice, and CD57 or HDD mice. These studies were non-GLP, PK and /or biodistribution studies and administered as single intravenous (IV) or subcutaneous (SC) dose.
L218: Preclinical biodistribution studies demonstrate that tebentafusp is being retained in the melanoma tumor for a significantly longer timeframe compared to other non-target tissues, with a half-life of tumour retention of approximately 24 hours.
L219: Observed pharmacokinetics for tebentafusp after IV administration were generally linear across dose levels tested in different strains of mice. Maximum concentrations (Cmax) and exposure (AUC) increased in an approximately dose proportional manner for dose levels from 0.005 to 0.05 mg/kg. The maximum concentrations were recorded at 15 minutes, as well as a secondary concentration peak at 1 hour, while the radioactivity was detectable in all samples up to 72 hours. The terminal half-lives calculated from the plasma radioactivity concentrations were also similar at each dose level. Tebentafusp exposure was maintained at measurable levels for 72 hours. Following SC administration of [^{3}H]-tebentafusp to CD57 mice at 0.05 mg/kg, the maximum mean plasma radioactivity concentration was lower and measured later than that measured after the IV dose route at the same dose level, as expected due to the dose route. Concentrations then decreased and were still detectable in all samples at 72 hours. The terminal half-life calculated from the plasma radioactivity concentrations was similar to that calculated following the intravenous administration.
L220: ## The FDA's Assessment:

L228: ## 5.5. Toxicology
L229: ## 5.5.1. General Toxicology
L230: ## The Applicant's Position:
L231: Tebentafusp can only recognize human HLA; therefore, all nonclinical toxicology studies were conducted in vitro. Human tissue and cell-based in vitro approaches were taken to understand potential organ-specific toxicities that may arise in the clinical setting with tebentafusp. These approaches considered the on-target/off-tumor potential effects, as well as the off-target/off-tumor effects (cross-reactivity) of tebentafusp. These studies are detailed in Secondary Pharmacology Section above.
L232: Tebentafusp was also investigated in toxicology studies to assess its cross-reactivity with normal tissues in a GLP-compliant tissue cross-reactivity study. The ability of tebentafusp to bind to a panel of 35 normal human tissues from 3 healthy donors was assessed using immunohistochemistry (IHC). Staining with tebentafusp was observed in the membrane and cytoplasm of lymphocytes throughout the human tissue panel examined. Similar staining was observed with the individual anti-CD3 component of tebentafusp, while no staining was observed with the individual TCR component, indicating that this staining was attributable to the binding of tebentafusp to CD3. As CD3 is a T lymphocyte marker, this staining was expected. No other staining was observed across the panel of tissues tested, indicating no unexpected cross-reactivity. It should be noted that the TCR targeting domain of tebentafusp recognizes very low target pHLA complexes (&gt;5 pHLA complexes per cell), which are expected to be outside the normal range of sensitivity of the IHC method and would therefore inform on cross-reactivity above the threshold of detection of IHC only. Overall, the results of this study are consistent with published reports of gp100 being specific to melanocytes and melanoma cells, and not in other.
L233: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L234: In general, and as noted by the Applicant, the use of IHC has limitations based on the low number of target complexes (i.e., IHC is not as sensitive a measure for this cross-reactivity assessment). Tebentafusp staining was observed in the membrane and cytoplasm of lymphocytes in the mammary gland, gastrointestinal tract (colon, esophagus, small intestine, and stomach), kidney, liver, lung, lymph node, fallopian tube, prostate, salivary gland, skin, spleen, thymus, thyroid, tonsil, ureter, urinary bladder, and cervix (Study IM 1861). FDA agrees that this appears to be attributable to CD3 binding. Of note, brain was negative for staining, indicating tebentafusp activity at &lt;1nM in 1/5 human astrocyte samples is likely of low clinical risk (Study IMC 1001).
L235: ## 5.5.2. Genetic Toxicology
L236: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L237: Tebentafusp can only recognize human HLA; therefore, all nonclinical toxicology studies were conducted in vitro. Therefore, no genetic toxicology studies were conducted. The indication is for patients with unresectable or metastatic uveal melanoma whose life expectancy is limited and the risk of death from their uveal melanoma outweighs the risk. This is consistent with requirements of ICH 6, and ICH 9.
L238: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L239: Based on the biologic properties of tebentafusp, a fusion protein, genetic toxicology studies are not warranted.
L240: ## 5.5.3. Carcinogenicity
L241: ## The Applicant's Position:
L242: Tebentafusp can only recognize human HLA; therefore, all nonclinical toxicology studies were conducted in vitro. Therefore, no carcinogenicity toxicology studies were conducted. The indication is for patients with unresectable or metastatic uveal melanoma whose life expectancy is limited and the risk of death from their uveal melanoma outweighs the risk. This is consistent with requirements of ICH 6, ICH 9, and S1.
L243: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L244: Carcinogenicity studies were not warranted for the proposed indication.
L245: ## 5.5.4. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
L246: ## The Applicant's Position:
L247: Tebentafusp is a bispecific fusion protein for the treatment of unresectable or metastatic uveal melanoma. The targeting end is an affinity-enhanced T cell receptor (TCR) that binds a human peptide fragment of the melanoma associated antigen glycoprotein 100 (gp100) presented by human leukocyte antigen-A*02:01 (HLA-A*02:01), which is expressed on the surface of melanoma cells. The effector end is a single chain variable fragment (scFv) that binds specifically to human CD3 on T cells. Once administered, tebentafusp is expected to bind to melanoma tissue and stimulate the immune system to attack the target tissue. Due to the specificity for human targets, embryo-fetal development (EFD) studies cannot be conducted in nonclinical species. Therefore, a weight of evidence assessment was conducted to evaluate the risk of tebentafusp administration on pregnancy and embryo-fetal development. A literature search revealed that gp100 is expressed embryonically in melanocytes found in the skin, inner ear, and eyes by 7 weeks of gestation, and MHC Class I classical proteins (including HLA-A*02:01) and transporter-associated-with-antigen-processing (TAP) proteins required for presentation of antigen on the cell surface are expressed very early in embryonic development. In addition, CD3-positive T cells begin to populate the periphery and are capable of being activated by around 12 weeks of gestation.
L248: However, tebentafusp's ability to directly affect embryo-fetal development requires that tebentafusp crosses the placenta and gains access to the fetus. The molecular weight of tebentafusp is 77 kDa, therefore, it is much too large to cross the placenta by diffusion. It also does not contain an Fc domain and, therefore, cannot bind to the neonatal Fc receptor (FcRn), which facilitates transfer of IgG molecules across the placenta, nor does it have domains that could bind to other placental transporters. Thus, tebentafusp would not be able to gain access to or elicit pharmacologic activity directly in the embryo/fetus. There is also no evidence that
L249: tebentafusp could interfere with implantation or the maintenance of pregnancy through its mechanism of action.
L250: Based on the results of this weight of evidence assessment, tebentafusp would not be expected to adversely affect embryo-fetal development or the maintenance of pregnancy when administered during pregnancy.
L251: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L252: Biologics of this molecular weight may cross the placenta. Based on its mechanism of action, tebentafusp may cause fetal harm, particularly the skin, inner ear, and eye, if the developing fetus is exposed to tebentafusp.
L253: ## 5.5.5. **Other Toxicology Studies**
L254: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L255: None conducted.
L256: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L257: No additional toxicology studies were warranted.

L262: ## 6 Clinical Pharmacology
L263: ## 6.1. Executive Summary
L264: Tebentafusp is a bispecific gp100 peptide-HLA-A*02:01 directed CD3 T cell engager. The applicant is seeking approval of tebentafusp for the treatment of HLA-A*02:01-positive adult patients with unresectable or metastatic uveal melanoma. The proposed dosing regimen is 20 mcg on Day 1, 30 mcg on Day 8, 68 mcg on Day 15, and 68 mcg once every week thereafter, via intravenous infusion over 15-20 minutes.
L265: The primary efficacy and safety evidence supporting the proposed dosage regimen results from the pivotal Phase 2 study IMCgp100-202, a randomized, open-label trial demonstrating improvements in overall survival (median of 21.7 months for Tebentafusp arm vs 16 month for Investigator's choice arm, with HR of 0.73 and p-value of 0.0139) and an overall acceptable safety profile in Tebentafusp arm.
L266: The dose selection is supported by the results from study IMCgp100-102 and study IMCgp100-01. Study IMCgp100-01 suggested that the flat dosing is preferable given more adverse events (AEs) observed in patients with higher body weight receiving body weight based dosing. Study IMCgp100-01 also showed no clinically meaningful differences in safety or efficacy between the weekly and daily dosing regimens. The maintenance dose selection is supported by the results from study IMCgp100-102, in which a higher 73 mcg dose was not well tolerated as indicated by elevations in hepatic transaminases with concurrent low-grade increases in bilirubin. No dose-limiting toxicities nor significant liver enzyme elevations were observed at the maintenance dose of 68 mcg.
L267: Cytokine release syndrome (CRS) is one of the most common adverse reactions, occurred in 89% of patients in Study IMCgp100-202 who received tebentafusp. CRS led to permanent treatment discontinuation in 1.2% of patients. The priming dosing strategy (20 mcg on Day 1, 30 mcg on Day 8) was adopted to mitigate CRS.
L268: Treatment-emergent anti-drug antibodies (ADA) against tebentafusp were detected in 33% and 29% of patients receiving tebentafusp across all doses in study IMCgp100-102 and study IMCgp100-202, respectively. The median onset time to ADA formation was 6-9 weeks after tebentafusp treatment. Tebentafusp concentration decreased in patients with ADA positivity. Ratio of tebentafusp concentration of ADA+/ADA- at cycles 5-13 ranged between 0.01 to 0.5. High-titer (&gt;Median titer of 8192) ADAs increased tebentafusp clearance by 35-fold. Exploratory subgroup analysis does not show evidence of clinically meaningful ADA impact on efficacy or safety. Information regarding the presence and impact by neutralizing ADA (Nab) on pharmacokinetics (PK), safety and efficacy was not included in current submission. A Post
L269: marketing commitment (PMC) will be issued to request assessing the presence of Nab in all patient samples that tested positive for binding antibodies and evaluating its clinical impact on PK, safety and efficacy.
L270: The clinical pharmacology review focused on the evaluation of dose selection and the clinical impact of immunogenicity on PK, safety and efficacy.
L271: ## 6.1.1. Recommendations:
L272: The Office of Clinical Pharmacology has reviewed the information submitted in BLA761228. This BLA is approvable from a clinical pharmacology perspective. The key review issues with specific recommendations/comments are summarized below in Table 5.
L273: Table 5: Key Clinical Pharmacology Review Issues and Recommendations by FDA

Pivotal and Supportive evidence of effectiveness	The primary evidence of effectiveness comes from the pivotal study IMCgp100-202. The proposed dosage regimen (20 mcg on Day 1, 30 mcg on Day 8, 68 mcg on Day 15, and 68 mcg once every week thereafter) is supported by improvements in overall survival (median of 21.7 month for Tebentafusp arm vs 16 month for Investigator's Choice arm), with HR of 0.51 and p value of < 0.0001), in HLA-A*02:01-positive adult patients with unresectable or metastatic uveal melanoma.

Weekly dosing: There are no clinically meaningful differences between weekly dosing as compared to daily dosing.
Priming dosing: Cytokine Release Syndrome (CRS) occurred during the first 3 weeks

L277: Information regarding the presence and impact by neutralizing anti-drug antibodies (Nab) on pharmacokinetics (PK), safety and efficacy was not included in the current submission. This following PMC is to be issued to request assessing the presence of Nab in all patient samples that tested positive for binding antibodies and evaluating its impact on PK, safety and efficacy.
L278: Conduct an assessment to determine the presence of neutralizing anti-drug antibodies against tebentafusp in all patient samples that tested positive for binding anti-drug antibodies in studies IMCgp100-202 and IMCgp100-102 by using the validated neutralizing anti-drug antibody assay. L289: ## The Applicant's Position:
L290: Noncompartmental analysis of tebentafusp from study IMCgp100-102 in mUM patients indicated dose-proportional PK with a terminal t1/2 of 6.8 to 7.5 hours. The estimated systemic CL and central volume of distribution of tebentafusp in melanoma patients were 4.33 L/d and 5.25 L, respectively. In population PK modeling, the eGFR and high-titer ADA (\ge median titer) were identified as significant covariates impacting CL.
L291: Exposure-response analysis revealed there were no clinically significant trends between tebentafusp exposure and key safety or efficacy endpoints over the dose range investigated (QW IV infusion of 5 ng/kg to 68 \mug of tebentafusp). In the final population PK model tebentafusp CL slightly decreased with decreasing renal function. However, CL values in renally impaired patients were essentially within the range observed in patients with normal renal function. Additionally, no impact on safety or efficacy parameters was identified in patients with mild to moderate renal impairment. Therefore, no dose adjustment is recommended in renally impaired patients. There is no information available in patients with severe renal impairment.

The results from these three studies generally provide adequate characterization of tebentafusp PK properties, exposure-response relationships and immunogenicity assessment. The Applicant's population PK (PPK) model is generally acceptable with minor change to the definition of immunogenicity. L323: ## The Applicant's Position:
L324: The recommended dosing regimen of tebentafusp is 20 mcg on Day 1, 30 mcg on Day 8, 68 mcg on Day 15, and 68 mcg once every week thereafter. Tebentafusp is administered via IV infusion over 15 to 20 minutes. The selection of intra-patient dose escalation over the first 3 weeks was
L325: identified in Study 01 and the actual doses were selected during the Phase 1 portion of Study 102. The final, recommended dosing regimen was then studied in the Phase 2 portion of Study 102 and the Phase 3 Study 202.
L326: **Study 01:** Several key observations in the first-in-human, Phase 1 study (Study 01) led to recommending an intra-patient escalation regimen using fixed doses. First, in Study 01, patients with higher body weights who received higher absolute doses of tebentafusp experienced more severe treatment-related toxicity. Consequently, fixed doses, rather than weight-based doses, were incorporated during the study. Second, given no clinically meaningful differences in safety or efficacy were identified between the weekly and daily dosing regimens, and in light of the easier utility of a weekly regimen, subsequent evaluation focused on weekly dosing. Third, the emerging safety profile of tebentafusp suggested that CRS (and associated hypotension) was more likely to occur during the first 2 weeks, after which tachyphylaxis occurred. Therefore, as has been used for other T cell-engaging bispecifics, an intra-patient escalation regimen during Weeks 1 and 2 (ie, 20 and 30 mcg, respectively) prior to dosing 50 mcg was selected as the preferred regimen to evaluate further in the subsequent single-arm, Phase 1/2 Study 102.
L327: **Study 102:** In Study 102, the intra-patient escalation regimen based on fixed dosing identified in Study 01 was further optimized by evaluating higher Week 3 doses (54 to 73 mcg) to achieve greater exposure. During the Phase 1 portion of this study, which employed a standard 3+3 dose-escalation design, the 73 mcg dose level was not tolerated due to elevations in hepatic transaminases with concurrent low-grade increases in bilirubin in 2 of 4 treated patients. These hepatic events were reversible without administration of corticosteroids, and all patients who experienced dose-limiting toxicities remained on treatment. After a dose reduction to 68 mcg, 6 patients were treated with no dose-limiting toxicities and no significant liver enzyme elevations. As a result, a dose of 68 mcg was identified as the maximum tolerated dose and selected for further evaluation as part of the intra-patient escalation regimen during the Phase 2 portion of the study.
L328: **Study 202:** The efficacy and safety of the intra-patient escalation regimen from Study 102, ie, 20 mcg on Day 1 and 30 mcg on Day 8, followed by the escalated dose of 68 mcg on Day 15 and weekly thereafter, were confirmed in the Phase 3 Study 202 in which all patients randomly allocated to tebentafusp received this regimen. In Study 202, this tebentafusp dosing regimen delivered convincing OS benefit and statistically significant PFS, and acceptable safety with a low discontinuation rate and no treatment-related deaths. Additionally, data from Study 102 revealed pharmacodynamic biomarkers consistent with the MoA including cytokine induction and T cell trafficking into the tumor.
L329: Blood samples were taken for PK assessment in patients at pre-dose-, the end of infusion, and 12 - 24 hours post end of infusion for each QW dose for the first cycle, pre-dose and at the end of infusion on the first dose of each cycle thereafter, and at the End of Treatment (EOT) visit.
L330: PK parameters calculated by noncompartmental methods from the Phase 1 dose escalation cohorts after dosing on C1D1 were comparable due to the dose range evaluated. Serum concentrations were measurable up to 24 hours after dosing but were below the LLOQ at subsequent timepoints
L331: between 24 and 168 hours. The C_{max} was achieved immediately after the end of infusion (median 0.5 hours), with a geometric mean C_{max} of 4063 pg/mL for C1D1 (20 mcg dose; Figure 11) and 13040 pg/mL for C1D15 (68 mcg dose; Figure 12). The increase in geometric mean PK exposure (C_{max}) of tebentafusp was approximately dose proportional between the 20 mcg dose administered on C1D1 and the 68 mcg dose administered on C1D15. From the dose escalation data, the observed terminal t_{1/2} was 6.8 to 7.5 hours. Subsequent mean C_{max} values after C1D15 ranged from 10000 to 20000 pg/mL, and mean pre-dose concentrations were undetectable, indicating no accumulation with QW dosing at the 68 mcg dose (Figure 13).
L332: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L333: FDA agrees with the Applicant's position that the proposed dosing regimen of tebentafusp for the general patient population (20 mcg on Day 1, 30 mcg on Day 8, 68 mcg on Day 15, and 68 mcg once every week thereafter, refer to as 20/30/68 mcg) is acceptable. The dosing regimen selection is based on the following rationale:
L334: - Priming dosing regimen: Cytokine release syndrome (CRS) mainly occurs within the first 3 weeks of dosing.
- Weekly dosing regimen: No clinically meaningful differences in safety or efficacy were identified between the weekly and daily dosing regimens. The weekly dosing regimen was adopted given the easier utility.
- Flat dosing regimen: Patients with higher body weights who received higher absolute doses of tebentafusp experienced more severe treatment-related toxicity. Analysis of PK data indicated that tebentafusp C_{max} is directly proportional to the absolute dose. As a result, heavier patients tended to have higher serum IMCgp100 concentrations. The data thus indicated that heavier patients were being unduly exposed to risk, which warranted a move from weight dosing to flat dosing.
- Maintenance dose of 68 mcg: 73 mcg was not well tolerated (elevations in hepatic transaminases with concurrent low-grade increases in bilirubin); furthermore, there were no dose-limiting toxicities and no significant liver enzyme elevations at the selected dosing regimen (20/30/68 mcg) in study 102 (N=6).
L335: Although only one priming dosing regimen (20 mcg on Day 1, 30 mcg on Day 8) was evaluated, the FDA considers further priming dose optimization effort is not necessary or feasible based on the following information:
L336: - The rate of grade 3+ CRS is low (0-0.5%).
- Grade 2 CRS was transient and mainly occurred during the first 3 weeks of dosing.
- Small number of patients (n=3) were treated with tocilizumab to mitigate CRS.
- The observed low ORR of 9% posts challenge for further priming dose optimization.

L353: ## The Applicant's Position:
L354: Effects on the PK of tebentafusp by the baseline characteristics were visually assessed by comparing the distributions of the interindividual variability estimates from the base PK model with tebentafusp. Baseline characteristics included, but were not limited to, age, weight, body mass index, eGFR, renal function, ADA titer, maximum ADA titer, ADA status, sex, race, melanoma stage, sum of tumor diameters, percent change from baseline sum of tumor diameters, and largest liver lesion group. Visual inspection of covariate versus eta (inter-individual variability) plots demonstrated that baseline renal function (eGFR) and ADA (titer&gt;8192) were the only statistically meaningful covariates on final PK model fit.
L355: Estimated tebentafusp CL slightly decreased (3.32 L/d) in patients with moderate renal impairment (eGFR ranging from 30 to 59 mL/min; Figure 14, Table 6). High interpatient variability was observed (78 %CV) and CL values in renal impaired patients were essentially within the range observed in patients with normal renal function. No impact on safety or efficacy parameters was identified in patients with mild to moderate renal impairment and no dose adjustments are recommended. There is no information available in patients with severe renal impairment (eGFR less than 30 mL/min).
L356: Estimated CL considerably increased when an ADA titer greater than 8192 was present (Figure 15; Table 6). However, ADA status was not associated with safety or efficacy and thus, monitoring of ADA is not necessary.
L357: Assessment of goodness of fit plots (Figure 16) was conducted to evaluate the performance of the final PK model. The model diagnostic plots indicate that the final PK model adequately describes the observed concentration data. No absolute value of conditional weighted residual error was greater than 4, and no recorded concentrations, were excluded based on poor fitting in model development.
L358: The PK of tebentafusp in patients with melanoma enrolled in these trials was adequately described by a two-compartment model with a first-order elimination from the central compartment. Only eGFR and the presence of high titer ADA were identified as relevant PK covariates. The estimated CL slightly decreased in patients with moderate renal impairment. However, CL values in renal impaired patients were essentially within the range observed in patients with normal renal function. Additionally, no impact on safety or efficacy parameters was identified. Therefore, no dose adjustment is recommended in renally impaired patients. Tebentafusp exposure was considerably decreased when an ADA titer greater than 8192 was present. Because ADA status was not associated with safety or efficacy of tebentafusp, monitoring of ADA is not necessary. Age, sex, weight, and race had no impact on the PK of tebentafusp in patients with melanoma.
L359: No dose modification are required for intrinsic or extrinsic factors.
L360: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L361: FDA agrees with the Applicant's position that no dose adjustment is necessary for patients with mild and moderate renal impairment although the estimated CL slightly decreased in this subpopulation.
L362: The tebentafusp clearance increased in patients with high titer ADAs. There was clear evidence of time-dependent impact of ADA on PK at the end of the infusion after Cycle 5. Mean Ratio of geometric mean tebentafusp concentration in ADA+/ ADA- patients during Cycle 6-13 in study 102 ranged between 0.01-0.55 and during cycle 5-13 for study 202 ranged between 0.01-0.5 (Figure 17). ADA status should be treated as time-varying covariates. Please refer to section 19.5.4 for details. The impact of ADA Titer \ge8192 on exposure was re-estimated and the USPI was updated as described in section 12.3. The figures (Figure 15, Figure 48) visualizing the ADA effect on PK provided by the applicant treated ADA status or titer as a fixed factor; therefore, FDA does not agree with the applicant's presentation of these data.
L363: **Figure 17. Tebentafusp concentration in ADA+/ ADA- Patients in Study 202**
L364: Source: FDA's independent analysis
L365: Exploratory subgroup analyses comparing the efficacy (OS, Figure 18) in patients who were ADA+ or ADA- did not show clinically significant differences.
L366: Figure 18. Association of Overall Survival with ADA Status, IMCgp100-202
L367: Source: Figure 44, summary of clinical pharmacology, page 80.
L368: Since ADA formation has the potential to induce type I-III hypersensitivity events, the impact of tebentafusp treatment emergent ADA on patient safety was evaluated using reported AEs from IMCgp100-102 and IMCgp100-202 filtered for hypersensitivity-associated events. No clinically relevant association was identified between hypersensitivity AEs and ADA onset (Table 8).

L380: ## 6.3. Comprehensive Clinical Pharmacology Review
L381: ## 6.3.1. General Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetic Characteristics
L382: ## Data:
L383: ## Lymphocyte Re-distribution
L384: As anticipated based on other T-cell engagers, blood lymphocyte levels decreased by 60% within 24 hours of the first dose of tebentafusp and returned to baseline by Day 8 after the first dose (Figure 19). This pattern was repeated after the third dose of tebentafusp on Day 15. Samples were not collected after the second dose (Day 8). These results are consistent with activation of lymphocytes followed by their margination and re-distribution into tumors and tissues.
L385: **Figure 19** Change in Blood Lymphocytes Over Time
L386: ## 1.1. Serum Biomarkers
L387: Consistent with other T cell engagers, all patients exhibited a treatment-induced increase in all cytokines and chemokines measured after the first dose. Levels of 11 immune markers associated with inflammatory responses (IFN-\gamma, tumor necrosis factor [TNF]-\alpha, IL-2, and IL-6), immune modulation (IL-10 and IL-1RA), and chemotaxis (C-X-C motif ligand [CXCL]9[MIG], CXCL10 [IP-10], CXCL11[I-TAC], hepatocyte growth factor (HGF), and monocyte chemoattractant
L388: protein-1 [MCP-1]) were measured in patient serum samples collected at baseline and on-treatment.
L389: Serum biomarker changes were transient, peaking at 8 to 24 hours post treatment. Biomarkers returned to baseline levels as measured in samples collected at Day 8, prior to patients receiving the second dose. The treatment-induced increase was seen again after the third dose, where the magnitude of increase was higher for certain markers, notably IL-10 and CXCL10 (Figure 20). Samples were not analyzed after the second dose (Day 8).
L390: **Figure 20** Change in Serum Cytokine Levels Over Time
L391: CXCL = C-X-C motif Ligand; D = Day; HGF = hepatocyte growth factor; IL = interleukin; MCP = monocyte chemoattractant protein; TNF = tumor necrosis factor
L392: ## 1.2. T-cell Infiltration into Tumors
L393: Consistent with other T-cell engagers, increases in CD3 (median 3.2-fold; Figure 21), CD8 (median 2.3-fold; Figure 22) and CD4 (median 2.1-fold; Figure 23) T cells within the tumor microenvironment were observed 16 days after the first dose. Biopsies were collected prior to treatment initiation and 16 days post first dose, (i.e., after the third dose) and analyzed for CD3, CD4 and CD8 T cells by immunohistochemistry. The majority of biopsies (68%) had a \ge 1.5-fold increase in CD3 T cells by Day 16, after 3 doses of tebentafusp.
L394: **Figure 21** CD3 T Cells Infiltrate Tumors after 3 Doses of Tebentafusp
L395: Left Panel: Pre-treatment; Right Panel: After 3 doses of tebentafusp
L396: **Figure 22** CD8 T Cells Infiltrate Tumors after 3 Doses of Tebentafusp
L397: Left Panel: Pre-treatment; Right Panel: After 3 doses of tebentafusp
L398: **Figure 23** CD4 T Cells Infiltrate Tumors after 3 Doses of Tebentafusp
L399: Left Panel: Pre-treatment; Right Panel: After 3 doses of tebentafusp
L400: ## 1.3. T-cell Activation and Down-regulation of Melanoma Genes in Tumor Biopsies
L401: As shown in Figure 24, consistent with the gp100-dependent activation of T cells by tebentafusp, gene expression analyses of paired baseline and on-treatment tumor biopsies collected at Day 16 revealed the following increases in messenger RNA (mRNA) levels after 3 doses of tebentafusp:
L402: - Interferon alpha (type 1 IFN) and gamma (type 2 IFN) pathway genes (2- and 3-fold respectively)
- HLA class 1, transporter associated with antigen processing (TAP)1, TAP2 and Proteasome 20S Subunit Beta 8 (PSMB8) (2-fold).
- Cytotoxic CD8 genes, granzyme B and perforin, that are required for tumor cell killing (2- to 3-fold).
L403: **Figure 24** Increased Expression of IFN alpha, IFN gamma, Granzyme B, Perforin 1, and Antigen Presenting Machinery in Tumor Biopsies by Day 16 After First Dose of Tebentafusp
L404: APM = antigen presenting machinery; GZMB = granzyme B; IFN = interferon; PRF1 = perforin 1; TPM = transcripts per million
L405: Taken together, these results demonstrate that tebentafusp induced a broad and potent anti-tumor immune response in metastatic uveal melanoma patients enrolled in the Phase 2 trial and support the proposed mechanism of action for tebentafusp.
L406: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L407: The mechanism of action and pharmacodynamic effects of tebentafusp were explored in the Phase 1 (Study IMCgp100-01, primarily cutaneous melanoma) and Phase 2 trials (Study IMCgp100-102). The analysis below is focused on Study IMCgp100-102 because it enrolled
L408: uveal melanoma patients who were treated at the 68 mcg dose after IE dose escalation, a regimen that was identical to that used in the Phase 3 study IMCgp100-202. Similar trends were observed in the Phase 1 trial (Middleton et al., 2020). The key pharmacodynamic effects of tebentafusp observed in study IMCgp100-102 are summarized here:
L409: - Tebentafusp induced transient reductions in blood lymphocyte levels which were accompanied by transient increases in serum inflammatory cytokines and chemokines.
- Tebentafusp increased CD3, CD4 and CD8 T cells levels in the tumor, consistent with T cell trafficking into the tumor microenvironment.
- Tebentafusp induced Type 1 (IFN\alpha) and Type 2 (IFN\gamma) interferon pathways in tumors.
L410: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L411: FDA considers the above biomarker assessments as supportive information to understand tebentafusp's mechanism of action.

L416: ## The Applicant's Position:
L417: Yes, the mechanism of action and pharmacodynamic effects of tebentafusp were explored in the Phase 1 (Study IMCgp100-01, primarily cutaneous melanoma) and Phase 2 trials (Study IMCgp100-102). The analysis below is focused on Study IMCgp100-102 because it enrolled uveal melanoma patients who were treated at the 68 mcg dose after IE dose escalation, a regimen that was identical to that used in the Phase 3 study IMCgp100-202. Similar trends were observed in the Phase 1 trial (Middleton et al., 2020). The key pharmacodynamic effects of tebentafusp observed in study IMCgp100-102 are summarized here:
L418: - Tebentafusp induced transient reductions in blood lymphocyte levels which were accompanied by transient increases in serum inflammatory cytokines and chemokines.
- Tebentafusp increased CD3, CD4 and CD8 T cells levels in the tumor, consistent with T cell trafficking into the tumor microenvironment.
L419: - Tebentafusp induced Type 1 (IFN\alpha) and Type 2 (IFN\gamma) interferon pathways in tumors.
L420: ## The FDA's Assessment:

L422: ## **6.3.4. Is the proposed dosing regimen appropriate for the general patient population for which the indication is being sought?**
L423: ## Data:
L424: ## The Applicant's Position:
L425: Justification for the Phase 3 tebentafusp dose and regimen is based on clinical data driven by safety, tolerability, and clinical activity from the dose escalation phases of study IMCgp100-01 and study IMCgp100-102 with supporting data from the expansion phase of study IMCgp100-102 rather than tebentafusp exposure-response. Validation of the tebentafusp regimen is provided by the Phase 3 results, including high OS (Hazard Ratio 0.51 for OS) and overall tolerability (low rate of tebentafusp-related discontinuations [3.1% Phase 2, 2.0% Phase 3] and no tebentafusp-related deaths).
L426: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L427: FDA agrees with the Applicant's position that the proposed dosing regimen of tebentafusp for the general patient population (20 mcg on Day 1, 30 mcg on Day 8, 68 mcg on Day 15, and 68 mcg once every week thereafter) is selected based on safety, tolerability and clinical activity from study IMCgp100-01 and study IMCgp100-102 and confirmed by the Phase 3 results.
L428: From a safety perspective, data from study IMCgp100-202 showed that CRS mainly occurred within the first 3 weeks of dosing, with the majority of the CRS events being grade 2 or below (Table 9). From an efficacy perspective, partial responses were observed in patients receiving 40 mcg weekly dosing and in 1 patient receiving 20 mcg IMCgp100 daily dosing, suggesting potential activity at 20-30 mcg dose.
L429: FDA considers that no further dose optimization on priming dose is considered necessary or feasible, given that CRS occurred during the first 3 weeks and with low rate of grade 3+ CRS (Table 9), and that the overall response rate (ORR) was low making the dose optimization effort challenging. In addition, a small number of patients (n=3) used tocilizumab, suggesting general
L430: acceptable tolerability (with respect to CRS).

L434: The selection of flat dosing strategy is based on result from study IMCgp100-01. The MTD for weekly tebentafusp dosing was identified as 600 ng/kg. Patients dosed with 600 ng/kg tebentafusp received an average dose of 49.3 mcg; however, experience with study drug identified the potential for higher serum concentrations to occur in heavier patients with body weight dosing. As a result, a flat dosing was selected over body weight based dosing.
L435: The selection of weekly dosing is supported by results from study IMCgp100-01 showing no clinically meaningful differences in safety or efficacy between the weekly and daily dosing regimens. From efficacy perspective, the profile of responses to tebentafusp treatment was comparable for patients treated with weekly dosing and daily dosing (9.3% vs 6.7%, Table 10). Partial responses were seen in 4 patients receiving 600 ng/kg and 1 patient receiving 40 mcg tebentafusp weekly dosing in Arm 1 and in 1 patient receiving 20 mcg tebentafusp daily dosing in Arm 2. In particular, all partial responses seen in patients with uveal melanoma were for those treated with the weekly dosing regimen. The overall response rate was 20.0% (3/15) (95% CI: 4.3% to 41.8%) for weekly dosing as compared to 0% (0/3) for daily dosing.

L443: The selection of the maintenance dose of 68 mcg dose is mainly based on results from study IMCgp100-102, in which the different maintenance dose was compared by evaluating fixed low doses of tebentafusp at C1D1 (20 mcg) and C1D8 (30 mcg), followed by an escalated cohort-specified QW dose administered at C1D15 and beyond (i.e., 54, 64, 73 and 68 mcg). Two out of four patients at the 20/30/73 mcg experienced dose limiting toxicities (DLT) (Grade 4 AST increased, and ALT increased in one patient and Grade 2 and Grade 3 AST increased and Grade 1 hyperbilirubinemia in another patient). All 3 patients recovered, and all resumed tebentafusp treatment at a reduced dose with no recurrence of toxicity. Given the DLTs reported at 73 mcg, a lower dose level of 68 mcg was tested. No DLTs were reported at this dose level, which was considered the MTD/RP2D for Phase 2 dose expansion.
L444: ## **6.3.2.2 Is an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy required for subpopulations based on intrinsic patient factors?**
L445: ## Data:
L446: See Therapeutic Individualization Section Above
L447: ## The Applicant's Position:
L448: No, See Therapeutic Individualization Section Above
L449: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L450: FDA agrees with the Applicant's position that no dose adjustment is needed in subpopulations based on intrinsic patient factors or ADA status. Although the ADA formation increased the tebentafusp clearance and decreased tebentafusp exposure, exploratory exposure-response analyses do not suggest any clinically meaningful differences in efficacy or safety between the ADA+ versus ADA- patient populations.
L451: ## **6.3.2.3 Are there clinically relevant food-drug or drug-drug interactions, and what is the appropriate management strategy?**
L452: ## **Data:**
L453: Not Applicable – iv infusion.
L454: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L455: Not Applicable – iv infusion.
L456: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L457: Tebentafusp can cause transient release of cytokines that may potentially suppress CYP450 enzymes. Based on evaluation of the totality of data, FDA considers the risk of drug interaction to be low. FDA's conclusion is based on the following considerations:
L458: - Tebentafusp's half-life of 6-8 hours is relatively short.
- The elevations of specific pro-inflammatory cytokines observed after the tebentafusp infusions are transient in nature, and attenuate with repeat dosing.
- The highest drug-drug interaction risk is following the first three doses of tebentafusp.
- Label recommends that patients are required to be observed after dosing for at least 16 hours, which alleviate the risk of potential drug interaction related AEs.
L459: This conclusion is supported by the observed clinical data. In study IMCgp100-202, twenty (20/392, 5.1%) tebentafusp-treated patients with metastatic uveal melanoma were identified as receiving concomitant medications with narrow therapeutic index within a 2-week period of a CRS episode. The rate of Grade 3 and 4 TEAEs in the selected population was comparable to the overall tebentafusp population and none of these patients had QTcF prolongation &gt; 500 msec during the susceptible period.
